So there is a significant difference there, isn&#8217;t there? That&#8217;s kind of interesting.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:06:20] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> Yeah.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:06:20] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Okay, we&#8217;ll dig into that in a moment. However, in the preparation for this podcast episode, Tiffany was very kind to send me three things.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">One of them was a YouTube video. It runs to about 20 minutes and sort of summarises what we&#8217;re going to talk about, I think, fairly well.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Another one was a blog post that you wrote in June of this year. It&#8217;s called Freedom is Not Free, A model for Open Source Sustainability. Again, very much aligned to what we&#8217;re going to talk about. That&#8217;s on palantir.net, which I&#8217;m at pains to point out is not the palantir that you are probably thinking about when you say the word palantir. And there&#8217;s another one which again, tackles the same subject, written broadly the same time. It was July this year, written by Dries, the founder of Drupal.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And what we&#8217;re trying to deal with here is, what I think Dries has summed up in the past is this maker, taker problem. And it&#8217;s very difficult to summarise. I must confess, I genuinely cannot articulate this problem. No matter how many times I&#8217;ve tried to articulate it to friends, to colleagues, to relations. At some point I get lost. At some point, the dynamics of it and the argument of it kind of boils boils down to it&#8217;s a moral thing.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So I&#8217;m just wondering, could you paint the picture of what you were trying to say in that YouTube video and in your blog post. What is the open source sustainability problem that you think, amongst many others, we have at the moment?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:07:47] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> We like to talk in open source about free and the four freedoms, especially for projects that are based in the GPL, right? But one of those freedoms, I think it comes down to the idea of the word free, right? There&#8217;s free is in speech. Where I am free to say what I want and when I want, and accept the consequences of that.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">But then there&#8217;s also this idea of free as in beer. And the reality is that the beer was never free. And the problem we have in open source is that for the last 25 years of Drupal, a really small group has been buying the rounds of beer for an entire ecosystem. And what I&#8217;m saying is that, beer has never been free. You just haven&#8217;t been taking your turn at buying the round, right?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So as we deal with kind of the pressures of the current economic situation, the uncertainty and opportunities created by AI, the number of people who are still buying those rounds is dwindling. But there&#8217;s still a lot of people who want to drink that free beer. There&#8217;s still a tonne of people who use WordPress, a tonne of people who use Drupal and get a lot of value out of it, right? They&#8217;re enjoying that.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">But what the problem is, is that we have this open source licence, and the licence is what conveys the freedom, right? It makes the code available. And in open source the more people who use the code, the cost of that code goes to zero. And no one&#8217;s use of the code is diminished by someone else also using that code.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">However, there&#8217;s more to being an open source project than just the code. And this is where the concept of stewardship comes in. Stewardship is what makes it dependable. It&#8217;s what has made it so that millions of people around the world can feel confident in downloading the software, using the software, hiring someone to create a WordPress or a Drupal implementation for you.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And that stewardship includes things like code hosting, or packaging. Testing frameworks. The security coordination. All of those things, which as more people use it become more expensive, not less.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So, there are these tensions around people who are getting value from open source software, but who currently don&#8217;t help pay for the cost that they impose on the project, largely because it&#8217;s invisible, right? So I think the first step is that we need to make those costs much more visible. So that&#8217;s something I&#8217;ve been working on in Drupal for a while. And so I want to expose it, right?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">We have 2,800 organisations in the last year who contributed into Drupal, but under 5% of them did two thirds of it, right? There are, I think as in WordPress, you know who really is driving and doing the work. And we do too, right? Obviously in Drupal we know who that is. And because we know who they are, and we know that they&#8217;re invested, and that they are passionate about open source and our project. They&#8217;re the ones who are easy to ask for financial contributions, or sponsorship to Drupal Con.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So you keep going back to the same pool. And in Drupal&#8217;s case, that means that 94% of our financial contribution comes from this tiny group of 5%. So they&#8217;re buying the rounds of beer for everyone. And that&#8217;s not a sustainable proposition.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:11:00] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Do you think, I&#8217;m going to rewind the clock a little bit. So, I regard the internet as an ongoing experiment. Where we are now is definitely not where it&#8217;s going to end up. And where we were 10 years ago, I don&#8217;t know that anybody would&#8217;ve predicted that we&#8217;d end up where we are today. You know, the internet came around and it evolved in the way that it did.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Do you see in the last, let&#8217;s say 25 years that many people have had access to the internet. Can you see moments where, and I&#8217;m doing air quotes, mistakes were made, which led to this problem? Or was it just happenstance and serendipity? We just got to this point? There&#8217;s no real pointing of a finger. There&#8217;s nobody to blame. There&#8217;s nobody to say, okay, you made dreadful mistakes. It&#8217;s just the way it evolved. There&#8217;s no question there, but do you understand what I&#8217;m saying?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:11:49] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> I do.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:11:50] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> With the benefit of hindsight, can you look back and point the finger, or is it more a case of okay, let&#8217;s just deal with the reality of where we&#8217;re at now.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:11:56] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> I can point to that moment. I was on the board at the time when Drupal moved over to Composer, right, and to modern packaging. And what I can tell you is that the board was never, wasn&#8217;t a big deal, right? They came to the board and I don&#8217;t even think we were really notified that this was happening or what it would mean.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And nobody modelled that packaging costs scaled to infinity, the more people who use it, right? There was no financial model put around it. We became a utility as opposed to just a repository. And I think that&#8217;s the moment for us that really changed things. You can continue to run events, Drupal Cons and Drupal Camps and all of that stuff. Those have pretty known economic models, and you can continue to run a website that promotes things. And you probably could even continue to be a collaboration platform.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">But once you start to become a packaging utility, then you become part of everyone&#8217;s infrastructure. And if you start to provide that for free, and people will expect it to continue to be free. And so I&#8217;m, really it&#8217;s only in the last couple of years that I&#8217;ve started to look into those costs. And for Drupal, we provide the Drupal Association itself provides $10 in services to every site, every year, every modern Drupal site every year.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:13:16] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Oh, that&#8217;s an interesting stat.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:13:17] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> It is.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:13:18] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> You&#8217;ve figured that out, you&#8217;ve done the maths and it costs $10 for every Drupal site that exists, whether or not they pay anything? That&#8217;s such an interesting number.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:13:26] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> Isn&#8217;t But I actually only take in $7.50. So I&#8217;ve been pushing forward in technical debt things for years. And that&#8217;s a risk, right? That&#8217;s a risk for me. And it&#8217;s also just unpleasant. I think that if we all start to become a little clearer around what the costs are as a utility, it&#8217;s actually not a lot.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">When it goes unpaid, it does have a cost, a compounding cost. And in Drupal&#8217;s history, what we used to say was if you want to support the project, support the conference. We all learned, obviously, during the pandemic that having an economic model based around a conference wasn&#8217;t the best choice.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I still think our ability to get together in person as a community is incredibly valuable, but I don&#8217;t see it as the way that we make the money to support the infrastructure. For a couple of reasons, right? One, it&#8217;s, not a charity. We are providing a service that people build businesses on, and they expect it to be there. But then also, especially in the case of Drupal, we have such a huge footprint in public sector work, and very large enterprise level government sites. And they do, they hit our infrastructure every 15 minutes, right?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Procurement has no way to pay either a community or a charity. There are operational roles that aren&#8217;t actually in someone&#8217;s self-interest to perform, or to underwrite, if you&#8217;re an organisation. And so quantifying it has been something I&#8217;ve been working on for the last couple of months. And so we&#8217;re working now with this $40 a year figure around the services that are provided in addition to the code, that we don&#8217;t have to, right? I imagine it maps broadly to the community that we&#8217;ve got. Do you find having these conversations is challenging? Let&#8217;s go with that word. What we were calling extractive use of open source.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So if you think of open source as just lumber that you can take and use however you want, right? We know that good use of open source, whether it is WordPress or Drupal or TYPO3, right? You want to minimise the amount of code that you own. That&#8217;s the advantage. That&#8217;s how we all experience the network effects.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So you need to have some behaviours, and I think that procurement officers don&#8217;t care, don&#8217;t need to know, about what open source is or our specific projects. But what they need to know is that when they are procuring any project, or any solution, that has open source as a component of it. That open source isn&#8217;t going to end up being code that they own down the road. They have the right to use it, extend it forever, and they know that somebody can&#8217;t turn it off on them, which is I think a very important.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:26:48] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Oh, big thing at the minute.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:26:49] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> Big thing right now, especially in light of the 2018 USA Cloud Act, right? So that&#8217;s really been the driving impetus around digital sovereignty. But when we think about good open source use, responsible open source use, there are some behaviours that regardless of project we know are true.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And it is that when you write a new plugin or module, you release it, right? So others can use it so that you&#8217;re sharing the maintenance burden moving forward. If you don&#8217;t, then you own that and you&#8217;re going to maintain those lines of code yourself.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Two, if you patch something, you want to release that patch upstream because otherwise you may have ended up inadvertently forking it. Or in some cases the government pays for the same patch over and over and over again, right? If there are economic benefit to using open source, you need to know that whatever vendor you&#8217;re using is contributing the patches back upstream so that you continue to stay, anything that&#8217;s non-confidential, obviously, but so that you can stay current and up to date, right? Woe for those who were not current and up to date last week with PHP, right? It made that problem all the more difficult.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So when I think about making it open source use at the government level very sustainable, there&#8217;s really three policies I think that they need to implement to help really get the benefits of open source, right?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">One is that the patches are contributed upstream if they&#8217;re non-confidential within 30 days.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Two, I think that they need to understand that there is a cost to their use. And so creating whether it&#8217;s 2% or 5%, creating a line item in every project that uses open source of any flavour, for the delivery of the project, that there is a standard percentage that is contributed back upstream to that open source project, to offset that use. Because right now, the agencies and the companies that are responsible open source users, they bear their contributions and their participation in open source as a tax. So it becomes baked into their hourly rates. Which then as economic conditions get constrained, it becomes a disadvantage to do the right thing for open source.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So I&#8217;m very worried about that. But if it is instead a flat line item that all who are responding to a tender have to meet, then the virtuous behaviour that continues the virtuous cycle of open source isn&#8217;t a burden on any one firm.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And then the third piece is you want to know, are they a contributor to that open source project? And so this speaks right to Dries&#8217; maker taker dilemma, which I&#8217;ve extended to the maker taker faker dilemma. Which is obviously, I think makers and takers are pretty clear, right? You either are someone who&#8217;s making and contributing into your open source project. And takers being the ones who are like, thanks for the wood little elves who magically made it in the woods. Great. This is all good. Good luck with that forest.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I think that the fakers are, in some ways, much more dangerous, because they have this, it&#8217;s like open source washing. The fakers are the ones who maybe sponsored a coffee one time at an event, but they&#8217;re not really giving things back. So they&#8217;ve got a badge. Procurement, they can say, oh look, I&#8217;m a sponsor.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I appreciate anyone who sponsors at any level. But I think that when you&#8217;re talking about competing on a public procurement, there is a very important difference between someone who maintains modules, contributes to the security team, and someone who sponsors a coffee. And I think that it&#8217;s on us as open source projects to make that clearer.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So the Drupal certification is our attempt to really become a very trusted public ledger about who&#8217;s contributing what, and why it matters to folks who aren&#8217;t in our community. I think today, where we stand and where we&#8217;re starting, it is a very transparent ledger. But there&#8217;s a difference between transparency and clarity. And I think if we want Brussels, and other governments and procurement, officers to be able to differentiate and give a preference to those who are good open source citizens, if you will, those who are actually involved in the projects that they&#8217;re using and implementing, for these very important public projects, I think it&#8217;s on us to make that easier. So that&#8217;s something I&#8217;ll be focused on next year.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:31:02] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> It sounds to me like you could kind of sum this up in the word clarity. Everybody needs clarity. Those people who are procuring things need to be clear on what they&#8217;re going to get. Those people who are delivering things need to be clear on what they&#8217;re going to do. And the notoriety, for want of a better word that they&#8217;re going to get from that.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">It also seems like you are in a very interesting role, where you are like this go-between between all the people doing the work on Drupal, and the people who are procuring, and I&#8217;m sure that&#8217;s really fascinating work. Like what is the proclivity of, let&#8217;s say for example, USA or Canada. What are the requirements that they have around, I don&#8217;t know, 20% of all public software needs to be open source or something like that. That must be utterly fascinating work. You&#8217;re right at the chalk face of all this. Wow, that&#8217;s so interesting.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:31:52] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> It&#8217;s absolute candy to my brain. Like I just, I can&#8217;t tell you how much I really am enjoying being part of those conversations. And I never would&#8217;ve thought that I would&#8217;ve been invited to speak at a plenary at the United Nations, and yet I was. And that blog post that you mentioned, that&#8217;s on palantir.net, actually those are remarks I gave to the German Consulate, in New York, right?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">It all comes from this conversation we&#8217;re all just starting to have about what does it mean to sustainably and responsibly use open source as we look ahead to the next 25 years, next a hundred years.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">There was a board member I had served with in Drupal a long time ago, Donna Benjamin, and this was, I don&#8217;t know, maybe almost 20 years ago now. And she always used to say, what&#8217;s our a hundred year plan? And at the time, I was taken back. I&#8217;m like, uh, I can&#8217;t even with that.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And now as we fast forward to where we are today, I actually can start to see that. And it&#8217;s about understanding that nobody intends and sets out to be a taker, right? You can&#8217;t pay for things that you don&#8217;t know are there, or things you don&#8217;t know are already happening. And you can&#8217;t meet expectations that aren&#8217;t communicated.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So this is the opportunity for open source projects to say, this is what it takes for us today. This is what it takes for us where we want to go. And here&#8217;s your share of it. How do we make it easy, and expected that everyone does a little, so we&#8217;re not resting on a few who have to do a lot?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:33:22] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> This is going to sound like a really poor summation of it, but it feels like open source, up until this point, you know, the analogy, carrot and stick, you know.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:33:30] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> Yes.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:33:30] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> So the carrot is, we hope that you&#8217;ll come along and, you know, follow the lead and do all of the right things. Where a stick is more, if you don&#8217;t do the right thing, there&#8217;ll be a punishment for that. It feels like open source for the last 20, 25 years, there&#8217;s been more carrot than stick. And although it&#8217;s incorrect to summarise this as stick, is there a little bit of stick here? Are we trying to cast a, let&#8217;s say, a glowing light on those that do everything well, who are the makers? You know, we want to raise your profile, shout from the rooftops that you did things well.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Conversely, is there a bit of okay, those that don&#8217;t, we are going to shame. Or is it more a case of we just leave you alone? You just don&#8217;t get a mention. You don&#8217;t get to be part of the community in quite the same way.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:34:15] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> Coming back to that frame as open source stewardship as a utility. Nobody considers it a donation to their electric company. They pay the bill because the lights matter. And I think it&#8217;s about resetting this frame. We&#8217;ve never sent utility bills to people. We&#8217;ve never even exposed what that looks like, or maybe we don&#8217;t know ourselves. Based on projects, Drupal certainly didn&#8217;t until I started this work.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So I think that there&#8217;s this expectation that we&#8217;re going to continue as we always have on this balance of goodwill and volunteerism. But as last week showed, PHP Foundation, which WordPress is very involved in, Drupal as well, has one full-time employee. And 70% of the web runs on it. So it&#8217;s something that we have taken for granted for too long. And I think that this moment is brokered by the blessing and the curse that is AI.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:35:07] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> I wonder if the wider issue at stake really is notifying the general public. So the Cyber Resilience Act in Europe is requiring SBOMs, which are Service Bill Of Materials to be disclosed. And I think that once SBOMs are readily available, it may become clearer, at least to decision makers, exactly how much open source software is in the solutions that you&#8217;re providing. And I really am curious to see how it evolves when you do have these manufacturers or organisations, whether they&#8217;re service organisations or actual manufacturers, starting to become liable for the risks in your security chain.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:36:56] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Right.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:36:56] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> As I look at this, I look at it as a supply chain issue. And so we&#8217;ve now exposed that there are weaknesses in our supply chain. So the argument isn&#8217;t, it&#8217;s really less of a shaming. It&#8217;s saying hey, this is a risk management approach. And I was looking at it as a design flaw. That open source was an idea around writing software. It wasn&#8217;t actually envisioned at any time that this was going to run the world. It was just, this is a good idea to do good way to do things. Let&#8217;s try it.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">But now that we understand that open source does run everything, we need to come back and treat this as a design problem and say, how do we align the incentives? What needs to be true?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I appreciate the work that&#8217;s going on in Europe right now, and I think that becomes a bit of the stick. It&#8217;s not coming from us. It&#8217;s saying, actually if you sell a product that&#8217;s based on open source, and that open source has a security issue, you are liable. You&#8217;re the manufacturer. See, as an open source steward, the Drupal Association does not have liability. We have responsibilities to disclose, which we have done, all the way back through 2007.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Drupal is a CNA, right? We&#8217;re a numbering authority for CVEs. We&#8217;ve had a very mature security practise for many years, and we will continue to do so, and meet our CRA disclosure requirements. But those who have blindly been using open source in products or in services for others, they&#8217;re now manufacturers. And come December of next year, they&#8217;re really going to start to understand how liable they&#8217;ve become for any issues in the supply chain.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:38:34] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> There&#8217;s something quite virtuous about that. In that it&#8217;s allowing the open source posture to carry on in the same way that it always has. Shifting the responsibility over to the governments in a way. You know, that they&#8217;ll create the legislation, which means that if you fall foul of this, that and the other thing, you will now be responsible. And if you are responsible, it&#8217;s it&#8217;s the same reason we have car insurance and buildings insurance. You need to know what your position is going to be in the event of a fire or a crash. You just have to know it. Because if your business were to go under because you weren&#8217;t thinking about those things, that would just be negligence. And so that&#8217;s such an interesting way of framing it. I like that a lot.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I also like your definition there. Let&#8217;s sell cheap lemonade.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">But then turning up with 12,000 people and saying, we would like 12,000 cheap lemonades, please. Immediately. No, that&#8217;s not available. That&#8217;s not the way it was designed. And that feels to have certain overlaps here.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Nobody knew that open source was going to take over the world. Now it has. It&#8217;s chronically underfunded. There will be a collapse at some point if companies and businesses are allowed to just have a taker mentality. So you are putting things in place to make sure that is checked. And I think that&#8217;s really interesting.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:40:06] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> It goes from this idea that we passively consume whatever we get. Whether it&#8217;s a website or a phone. To this idea of having some sort of active upstream strategy. And again, I don&#8217;t think that many of us had the opportunity to understand what software was running on our phone. You didn&#8217;t know, right? You can&#8217;t mitigate a risk that you don&#8217;t know is there. I do think it is unreasonable to think that we don&#8217;t know the risk is there now, especially after last week.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:40:33] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> There&#8217;s just no bit of me which would walk into a store and pick things up and just wander out and say. So, Tiffany Farriss, thank you so much for making the time to chat to me today.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:48:16] <strong>Tiffany Farriss:</strong> Nathan, thank you for having me.</p>\n</div></details>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">On the podcast today we have <a href=\"https://www.drupal.org/u/farriss\">Tiffany Farriss</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Tiffany has spent the last 30 years working in web design, development, and creation, and she’s been deeply embedded in the Drupal community for the past two decades. She’s currently the interim CEO of the Drupal Association, and is the organisation’s longest-serving board member after Drupal’s founder, Dries Buytaert.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">This episode is a bit of a departure, as we usually focus squarely on WordPress. But Tiffany brings a wealth of experience from the broader PHP family, and this conversation explores the similarities and differences between the WordPress and Drupal ecosystems, particularly when it comes to infrastructure, and the sustainability of open source projects.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Tiffany explains the ‘maker-taker’ problem and the challenges of sustaining free software platforms that underpin millions of sites across the web. She shares her insights on the real costs behind open source stewardship, drawing from both data and experience, and discusses how tiny groups have shouldered the burden for everyone, and why that might no longer be viable.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The discussion explores the Drupal Certified Partner program, the push towards quantifying contributions, and how the open source community can improve transparency and sustainability. Tiffany also touches on the role of public sector procurement in shaping the future of open source, the impact of new regulations, and how community-driven recognition could incentivise more organisations to ‘buy their round of beers’ for the ecosystem.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you’re interested in the sustainability of open source, the evolution of governance models, and how WordPress professionals can learn from the broader PHP landscape, this episode is for you.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Useful links</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><a class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress 7.2 Beta 5 is ready for download and testing!</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>This beta release is intended for testing and development</strong> only. Please do not install, run, or test this version of WordPress on production or mission-critical websites. Instead, use a test environment or local site to explore the new features.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">How to Test WordPress 7.2 Beta 5</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">You can test WordPress 7.2 Beta 5 in any of the following ways:</p>\n\n\n\n<figure class=\"wp-block-table\"><table class=\"has-fixed-layout\"><tbody><tr><td><strong>WordPress Beta Tester Plugin</strong></td><td>Install and activate the <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/plugins/wordpress-beta-tester/\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\" target=\"_blank\">WordPress Beta Tester</a> plugin on a WordPress install. Select the “Bleeding edge” channel and “Beta/RC Only” stream.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Direct Download</strong></td><td>Download the <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/wordpress-7.2-beta5.zip\">Beta 5 version (zip)</a> and install it on a WordPress website.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Command Line (WP-CLI)</strong></td><td>Use this <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/cli/\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\" target=\"_blank\">WP-CLI</a> command: <br /><code>wp core update --version=7.2-beta5</code></td></tr><tr><td><strong>WordPress Playground</strong></td><td>Use a <a href=\"https://playground.wordpress.net/release\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">7.2 Beta 5 WordPress Playground instance</a> to test the software directly in your browser. No setup required-just click and go!</td></tr></tbody></table></figure>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The scheduled final release date for WordPress 7.2 is <strong><strong>December 8, 2026</strong></strong>. The full <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/10/06/wordpress-7-2-release-party-schedule/\">release schedule can be found here</a>. Your help testing Beta and RC versions is vital to making this release as stable and powerful as possible. Thank you to everyone who contributes by testing!</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">What&#8217;s in WordPress 7.2 Beta 5?</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">For technical details on the issues addressed since Beta 4, see the following links:</p>\n\n\n\n<ul class=\"wp-block-list\">\n<li><a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/query?status=closed&amp;changetime=11%2F03%2F2026..11%2F10%2F2026&amp;milestone=7.2&amp;group=component&amp;col=id&amp;col=milestone&amp;col=owner&amp;col=type&amp;col=priority&amp;order=id\">Closed 7.2 WordPress Core Trac tickets</a> since November 3, 2026</li>\n\n\n\n<li><a href=\"https://github.com/WordPress/gutenberg/commits/wp/7.2?since=2026-11-03&amp;until=2026-11-10\">7.2 Gutenberg commits</a> since November 3, 2026</li>\n</ul>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Continued Improvements to WordPress 7.2</h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress 7.2 Beta 5 continues refining the upcoming release with additional security, accessibility, and compatibility improvements.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Recent changes include email notifications when application passwords are created, improved keyboard navigation in the WordPress admin toolbar, and accessibility refinements to Visual History. Additional fixes strengthen permissions for Notes reactions, improve compatibility with MP3 uploads, and address automatic update behavior in Git worktree installations.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Learn more:</strong> <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/63927\">Trac #63927</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66331\">Trac #66331</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/65122\">Trac #65122</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66447\">Trac #66447</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/46544\">Trac #46544</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66460\">Trac #66460</a></p>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Further Refinements to Ipsum</h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The new default theme, Ipsum, receives another round of improvements in WordPress 7.2 Beta 5. Accessibility enhancements include improved keyboard focus indicators and view transitions that respect reduced-motion preferences. Additional refinements to block styles and patterns provide greater design flexibility, while optimized stylesheets and other under-the-hood improvements help prepare the theme for its final release.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Learn more:</strong> <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66631\">Trac #66631</a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/WordPress/ipsum/pull/198\">GitHub #198</a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/WordPress/ipsum/pull/187\">GitHub #187</a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/WordPress/ipsum/pull/180\">GitHub #180</a></p>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\">PHP 8.6 Compatibility Improvements</h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress 7.2 aims to fully support PHP 8.6, which is scheduled to be released on November 19, 2026. The WordPress project has a long history of being compatible with new versions of PHP as soon as possible, and this release is no different.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">As of the Beta 5 release, all known compatibility issues with PHP 8.6 have been resolved.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">While all of the known issues in WordPress have been addressed, you will need to verify that all of your plugins and themes are also compatible with PHP 8.6 prior to upgrading. Keep an eye on the Making WordPress Core blog in the coming days for more detailed information about what to look for.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress continues to encourage all users to run the latest and greatest versions of PHP.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Learn more:</strong> <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66648\">Trac #66648</a></p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">How important is your testing?</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Testing for issues is a critical part of developing any software, and it’s a meaningful way for anyone to contribute – whether or not you have experience. <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/test/2026/10/20/help-test-wordpress-7-2/\">Details on what to test in WordPress 7.2 are available here</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you encounter an issue, please share it in the <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/support/forum/alphabeta/\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">Alpha/Beta area</a> of the support forums. If you are comfortable submitting a reproducible bug report, you can do so <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/newticket\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">via WordPress Trac</a>. You can also check your issue against this list of <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/tickets/major\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">known bugs</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Curious about testing releases in general and how to get started? Follow along with the <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/test/\">testing initiatives in Make Core</a> and join the <a href=\"https://wordpress.slack.com/messages/core-test/\">#core-test channel</a> on <a href=\"https://wordpress.slack.com/\">Making WordPress Slack</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">A Beta 5 haiku</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><em>One last beta blooms<br />Release candidate awaits<br />The finish draws near</em></p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"has-text-align-right wp-block-paragraph\"><em>Props to <a class=\"mention\" World\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:4:\"guid\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:34:\"https://openchannels.fm/?p=2566511\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:4:\"link\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:68:\"https://openchannels.fm/defaults-and-why-they-quietly-run-the-world/\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:11:\"description\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:111:\"A Wrigley\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:7:\"pubDate\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:31:\"Tue, 10 Nov 2026 11:04:00 +0000\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}}s:32:\"http://purl.org/dc/elements/1.1/\";a:1:{s:7:\"creator\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:8:\"Bob blog: WordPress Credits Welcomes More Than 50 Institutions\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:4:\"guid\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:35:\"https://wordpress.org/news/?p=21881\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:4:\"link\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:57:\"https://wordpress.org/news/2026/11/wp-credits-reaches-50/\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:11:\"description\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:3386:\"<figure class=\"wp-block-image size-large\"><img alt=\"\" class=\"wp-image-21882\" height=\"576\" src=\"https://i0.wp.com/wordpress.org/news/files/2026/10/Upside-down-lightbulb-sketches-in-notebook.png?resize=1024%2C576&#038;ssl=1\" width=\"1024\" /></figure>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">More than 50 educational institutions have joined <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/education/credits/\">WordPress Credits</a>, giving students worldwide a way to earn academic recognition for contributing to WordPress. The program had two participating institutions in September 2025 and four the following month. By June 2026, that number had reached 27, before passing 50 this October.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The program launched with the University of Pisa and was announced at WordCamp Europe 2025. Since then, it has expanded through partnerships that connect students with experienced contributors and practical projects. The <a href=\"https://wordpress.github.io/WPCredits/\">WordPress Credits dashboard</a> recorded participating institutions across 17 countries as of October 19, including Bangladesh, Costa Rica, Spain, Bolivia, and Indonesia. Each institution can adapt the program to its academic requirements, allowing students to contribute as part of their studies.</p>\n\n\n\n<figure class=\"wp-block-image size-large\"><img alt=\"\" class=\"wp-image-21883\" height=\"431\" src=\"https://i0.wp.com/wordpress.org/news/files/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-23-at-1.16.32-pm.png?resize=1024%2C431&#038;ssl=1\" width=\"1024\" /></figure>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Learn through contribution</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Students work with mentors on projects that reflect their interests and areas of study. They might translate WordPress, improve documentation, create learning materials, or contribute to development and testing. Alongside that work, they practice collaborating with others and responding to feedback, then present their contributions at the end of the program. By October 19, the dashboard recorded 364 graduates and student contributions across 18 WordPress teams. That work supports the software and resources people use throughout the WordPress community.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Help the program grow</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Local contributors have helped establish these partnerships by introducing WordPress Credits to nearby schools and adapting it to local languages and educational settings. Mentors guide students through their first contributions, while sponsors provide support and resources. Educators help make that participation part of students’ academic work. Thank you to the students, educators, mentors, and supporters whose involvement has brought the program to more than 50 institutions.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Institutions can include WordPress Credits in an existing course, offer it as an internship, or incorporate it into a final project. The program is available at no cost to partner institutions, with flexible formats to suit their students and curriculum. To get started, read <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/community/handbook/education/credits/how-to-participate/institutions/\">how to launch a WordPress Credits program</a> and <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/education/credits/\">register your institution’s interest through the program page</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"></p>\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:7:\"pubDate\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:31:\"Mon, 09 The October 28 security release fixes more than a dozen issues, including a weakness in the upgrade process that could lead to remote code execution and stored XSS in several blocks and the Interactivity API. The fixes were backported all the way to the 4.7 branch, so don&#8217;t forget about those older sites you maintain.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Running a store? WooCommerce had a busy week, too. <a href=\"https://developer.woocommerce.com/2026/11/04/woocommerce-11-2-2-release-notes/\"><strong>WooCommerce 11.2.2</strong></a> is a security release with a database update, and there&#8217;s a separate <a href=\"https://developer.woocommerce.com/2026/11/03/security-update-for-woopayments/\"><strong>security update for WooPayments</strong></a> with a table of fixed versions for each release line.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">As for me, I&#8217;m just enjoying the autumn weather. It feels like we&#8217;ve finally shed off the days of summer heat in Alabama. And that means it&#8217;s fall festival season. I&#8217;ll see if I can find one or two to enjoy in the coming weeks.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">With that, let&#8217;s get down to the latest news.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Your friendly neighborhood developer advocate, <img alt=\"🍂\" class=\"wp-smiley\" src=\"https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/17.0.2/72x72/1f342.png\" style=\"height: 1em;\" /><br /><em>Justin Tadlock</em></p>\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-newsletterglue-showhide ng-block\" width=\"100%\">\n<div class=\"wp-block-group has-accent-5-background-color has-background href=\"https://gutenbergtimes.com/feed/#ai-in-wordpress\">AI in WordPress</a></li></ul></nav></div>\n</div>\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"0-word-press-release-information\">Developing Gutenberg and WordPress</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress 7.2 is one step closer. <strong>David Baumwald</strong> announced <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/news/2026/11/wordpress-7-2-beta-4/\"><strong>WordPress 7.2 Beta 4</strong></a> on November 3. Beta 3 shipped inside the 7.1.4 security release. This round continues the work on the Abilities API and DataViews extensibility, and it improves focus visibility in Windows High Contrast mode. Translation updates also show up individually on the Updates screen now, so you can choose which ones to install. <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/news/2026/10/wordpress-7-2-beta-2/\"><strong>Beta 2</strong></a> laid the groundwork for much of this, adding server-side registration for DataViews and DataForm and moving the admin to SVG icons.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The final release is still set for December 8. Spin up the <a href=\"https://playground.wordpress.net/release\">Beta 4 Playground instance to test</a>, or run <code>wp core update --version=7.2-beta4</code> on a staging site.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Formatting no longer stops at the edge of a block. In <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/11/02/whats-new-in-gutenberg-24-3-28-october/\"><strong>What&#8217;s new in Gutenberg 24.3</strong></a>, <strong>George Mamadashvili</strong> shows how you can now select text across several blocks and format all of it at once. Notes pick up emoji reactions, and the Media inserter moves onto the same DataViews component behind the Site Editor&#8217;s lists. Failed saves finally explain themselves, too, with a copy button for sharing the details. The release carries the security fixes from 7.1.4, so update the plugin if you run it. The 24.4 release candidate followed on November 3.</p>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"getting-ready-for-wordpress-7-2\"><strong>Getting ready for WordPress 7.2</strong></h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Dashicons are on their way out of the admin bar and menu. In WordPress 7.2, those icons are inline SVGs, and <strong>Ashar Fuadi</strong> explains <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/11/05/svg-icons-in-the-admin-bar-and-menu-in-wordpress-7-2/\"><strong>what the SVG icon change means for plugins and themes</strong></a>. Most code needs nothing. But admin color schemes and anything that styles or hides icons through <code>::before</code> will need a small CSS update, and the post has before-and-after rules you can copy. There&#8217;s a new API, too: <code>add_menu_page()</code>, <code>register_post_type()</code>, and admin bar nodes now accept a registered icon name like <code>core-admin/pin</code>.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Visit <code>/random/</code> on a 7.2 site, and you&#8217;ll land on a random published post. <strong>Aaron Jorbin</strong> details <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/11/05/your-content-is-going-to-get-more-random/\"><strong>the new random content endpoint</strong></a>, which also works for categories, tags, authors, and custom post types. It&#8217;s on by default, with a new <code>randomable</code> argument for post types and a <code>wp_enable_random_content_redirect</code> filter to switch it off.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">It hasn&#8217;t landed quietly. Dozens of comments push back on the on-by-default choice, citing hidden content that could surface (Core still has no <code>noindex</code> option), <code>ORDER BY RAND()</code> load on large sites, and agencies now facing an must-use plugin across hundreds of sites. <strong>Matt Mullenweg</strong> has since agreed it shouldn&#8217;t use the page URL structure, so expect changes before 7.2 ships.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Anne McCarthy</strong> pulled several features still in development into one prototype for <a href=\"https://nomad.blog/2026/11/02/imagining-the-near-future-of-collaboration-in-wordpress/\"><strong>imagining the near future of collaboration in WordPress</strong></a>. It combines the 7.2 Notes features, suggestion mode, and the Presence API with a small workflow plugin that blocks publishing until every open note and suggestion is resolved. She purposely left real-time collaboration out to show how much works without it. She also shares the full prompt she used. Borrow it and build the demo yourself. Watch for an upcoming call for testing on suggestions.</p>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"feature-notifications-get-a-design-and-some-pushback\"><strong>Feature Notifications get a design, and some pushback</strong></h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Plugins have spent years shouting over each other with admin notices. <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/10/29/exploring-a-design-for-feature-notifications/\"><strong>Exploring a design for Feature Notifications</strong></a>, from <strong>Poli Gilad</strong>, proposes a single notification bell on every admin screen, in the editor, and on the front of the site, with plugins sending data rather than layout. Existing admin notices keep working.</p>\n\n\n\n<figure class=\"wp-block-image size-full has-custom-border\"><img alt=\"Screenshot of proposed feature notifications dropdown with tabs for different types of notifications.\" class=\"has-border-color has-accent-6-border-color wp-image-47434\" height=\"1338\" src=\"https://i0.wp.com/gutenbergtimes.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/feature-notifications-proposal.webp?resize=1776%2C1338&#038;ssl=1\" style=\"border-width: 1px;\" width=\"1776\" /></figure>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">That last point drew the most criticism. <strong>Matt Mullenweg</strong> argued that the proposal does nothing about today&#8217;s notice spam and that the fix needs directory guidelines, enforcement, and incentives, not only a new interface. He backed <strong>David Perez&#8217;s</strong> suggestion that plugins declare each notice&#8217;s intent (operational, setup, or promotional) as a requirement.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">On the code side, <strong>Jonathan Bossenger&#8217;s</strong> <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/11/01/wp-feature-notifications-weekly-update-2026-10-30/\"><strong>weekly update for October 30</strong></a> reports that the notification repository is now scoped per user, laying the groundwork for the REST API. Prefer to watch the work happen? His <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Qt6zujtlBVk\"><strong>Friday live stream</strong></a> includes testing the plugin in Studio and the AI-assisted workflow he uses for reviewing PRs. He also holds office hours in the Feature Notifications Slack channel on Thursdays at 10:00 UTC.</p>\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-newsletterglue-showhide ng-block\" width=\"100%\">\n<figure class=\"wp-block-embed is-type-video is-provider-youtube wp-block-embed-youtube wp-embed-aspect-16-9 wp-has-aspect-ratio\"><div class=\"wp-block-embed__wrapper\">\n\n</div></figure>\n</div>\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"prototypes-from-dave-on-wp\"><strong>Prototypes from Dave on WP</strong></h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The block toolbar has picked up a lot of buttons over the years. <strong>Dave Smith</strong> asks <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Hja-5ercQ-Q\"><strong>whether this is the end of the block toolbar</strong></a> and demos a consolidated version inside his Create Not Learn prototype editor. In about eight minutes, you&#8217;ll see how grouping those controls could make everyday formatting quicker and more consistent without giving up any of the editor&#8217;s power. The video description has a Playground link, so you can click around it yourself.</p>\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-newsletterglue-showhide ng-block\" width=\"100%\">\n<figure class=\"wp-block-embed is-type-video is-provider-youtube wp-block-embed-youtube wp-embed-aspect-4-3 wp-has-aspect-ratio\"><div class=\"wp-block-embed__wrapper\">\n\n</div></figure>\n</div>\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">He also took a <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nZNfuQSxyN0\"><strong>first look at drag-and-drop grid building</strong></a>, pulling Andrei Draganescu&#8217;s work-in-progress free-form grid into the same prototype. You can drag, resize, snap, and rotate blocks on a grid, and that demo has its own Playground link.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Release roadmap posts have kept roughly the same shape for three years. <strong>Anne McCarthy</strong> wants to know whether they still work, and she&#8217;s asking everyone from Core committers and extenders to hosts and everyday users to <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/10/26/release-roadmap-survey/\"><strong>help shape future release roadmaps</strong></a> by November 16.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"0-p\">Plugins, Themes, and Tools for #nocode site builders and owners</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress Studio is now built around a conversation. <strong>Rich Tabor</strong> introduced <a href=\"https://wordpress.com/blog/2026/11/04/wordpress-studio-workspace/\"><strong>a new workspace for building with WordPress</strong></a>, where you describe the site you want and an AI agent builds a first version next to a live preview. It offers four looks, then four layouts, and saves your choices to a <code>DESIGN.md</code> file so later chats keep the same design. You can pin notes on the preview with Annotate, bring your own Anthropic API key, or connect Claude Code, Codex, or Cursor over MCP. It&#8217;s a free desktop app for macOS, Windows, and Linux.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">For a hands-on take, <strong>Elliott Richmond</strong> writes up <a href=\"https://elliottrichmond.co.uk/wordpress-studio-new-workspace-is-out-whats-changed-since-my-first-look/\"><strong>what&#8217;s changed since his first look</strong></a>, along with one fair caution: the agent can now edit your live site directly, so building locally first is still the safer habit. Rich also shared <a href=\"https://richtabor.com/studio/\"><strong>why he rebuilt Studio</strong></a> on his own blog, and <strong>Nick Diego</strong> recorded a <a href=\"https://x.com/nickmdiego/status/2118020671938900064\"><strong>quick video overview</strong></a> of the new design and agentic features.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Stores running WooCommerce&#8217;s Purple theme are about to get more design control. In the <a href=\"https://developer.woocommerce.com/2026/10/26/woocommerce-11-3-pre-release/\"><strong>WooCommerce 11.3 pre-release post</strong></a>, <strong>Brian Coords</strong> highlights color, spacing, and border controls curated for each block, including Product, Featured Product, Featured Category, and Product Categories List. The On-Sale Badge block can show the discount as an amount, a percentage, or custom text, and Product Gallery swiping on mobile now follows your finger naturally. The beta is out now, with the final release scheduled for the week of November 9.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Two new short tutorials from WordPress.com cover blocks you&#8217;ll use on nearly every site. The <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Zi9r3kmfehI\"><strong>Columns block walkthrough</strong></a> builds grids, feature sections, and pricing tables, including column widths and stacking on mobile. </p>\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-newsletterglue-showhide ng-block\" width=\"100%\">\n<figure class=\"wp-block-embed is-type-video is-provider-youtube wp-block-embed-youtube wp-embed-aspect-16-9 wp-has-aspect-ratio\"><div class=\"wp-block-embed__wrapper\">\n\n</div></figure>\n</div>\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The <a href=\"https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p7H59fW4_U0\"><strong>Query Loop block video</strong></a> shows how to choose which posts appear, then sort and filter them, in about two minutes. Bookmark both for the next time a client asks how it&#8217;s done.</p>\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-newsletterglue-showhide ng-block\" width=\"100%\">\n<figure class=\"wp-block-embed is-type-video is-provider-youtube wp-block-embed-youtube wp-embed-aspect-16-9 wp-has-aspect-ratio\"><div class=\"wp-block-embed__wrapper\">\n\n</div></figure>\n</div>\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"2-word-press-6-0-1-and-6-1-scheduled\">Theme Development for Full Site Editing and Blocks</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Ipsum, the default theme shipping with WordPress 7.2, is easy to mistake for a simple theme. <strong>Carlo Daniele&#8217;s</strong> <a href=\"https://kinsta.com/blog/ipsum-wordpress-theme/\"><strong>dive into the Ipsum theme and how to extend it</strong></a> for Kinsta shows what&#8217;s underneath. Templates hand their layouts to hidden patterns, around 60 patterns ship with it, and the default text colors reach a WCAG AAA contrast ratio. </p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">My favorite detail is its use of the Block Bindings API: a comments call to action changes its text depending on whether the post already has comments.</p>\n\n\n<table cellpadding=\"0\" cellspacing=\"0\" class=\"ng-block-7a6622d3928e4c63 wp-block-newsletterglue-container ng-block\" style=\"color: #666666;\" width=\"100%\">\n<tbody>\n<tr>\n<td class=\"ng-block-vs ng-block-vs-1\" colspan=\"3\" height=\"0\" style=\"height: 0px;\"></td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td class=\"ng-block-hs ng-block-hs-1\" height=\"0\" style=\"width: 0px;\"></td>\n<td align=\"none\" class=\"ng-block-td\" style=\"font-size: 16px; font-family: Helvetica; line-height: 1.6; font-weight: normal; padding-top: 20px; padding-bottom: 20px; padding-left: 0px; padding-right: 0px; text-align: none; color: #666666; background-color: #fdfcea; border-radius: 12px;\">\n<table cellpadding=\"0\" cellspacing=\"0\" class=\"wp-block-newsletterglue-text ng-block\" style=\"color: #666666;\" width=\"100%\">\n<tbody>\n<tr>\n<td align=\"none\" class=\"ng-block-td\" style=\"font-size: 16px; font-family: Helvetica; line-height: 1.6; font-weight: normal; padding-top: 8px; padding-bottom: 10px; padding-left: 20px; padding-right: 20px; text-align: none; color: #666666;\">\n<p><strong> <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/handbook/references/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index/\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\" target=\"_blank\">&#8220;Keeping up with Gutenberg &#8211; Index 2026&#8221;</a> </strong><br />A chronological list of the WordPress Make Blog posts from various teams involved in Gutenberg development: Design, Theme Review Team, Core Editor, Core JS, Core CSS, Test, and Meta team from Jan. 2024 on. Updated by yours truly. </p>\n</td>\n</tr>\n</tbody>\n</table>\n<table cellpadding=\"0\" cellspacing=\"0\" class=\"wp-block-newsletterglue-text ng-block\" style=\"color: #666666;\" width=\"100%\">\n<tbody>\n<tr>\n<td align=\"none\" class=\"ng-block-td\" style=\"font-size: 16px; font-family: Helvetica; line-height: 1.6; font-weight: normal; padding-top: 8px; padding-bottom: 10px; padding-left: 20px; padding-right: 20px; text-align: none; color: #666666;\">\n<p>The previous years are also available: <br /><strong><strong><a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/handbook/references/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index-2020/\">2020</a> | <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/handbook/references/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index-2021/\">2021</a></strong> | <strong><a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/handbook/references/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index-2022/\">2022</a></strong></strong> | <strong><a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/handbook/references/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index/gutenberg-index-2023\">2023</a></strong> | <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/handbook/references/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index/gutenberg-index-2024/\"><strong>2024</strong></a> | <strong><a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/handbook/references/keeping-up-with-gutenberg-index/gutenberg-index-2025/\">2025</a></strong></p>\n</td>\n</tr>\n</tbody>\n</table>\n</td>\n<td class=\"ng-block-hs ng-block-hs-2\" height=\"0\" style=\"width: 0px;\"></td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td class=\"ng-block-vs ng-block-vs-2\" colspan=\"3\" height=\"0\" style=\"height: 0px;\"></td>\n</tr>\n</tbody>\n</table>\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"3-building-themes-for-fse-and-word-press\">Building Blocks and Tools for the Block editor.</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">PHPStan can&#8217;t check a call to a class it has never seen, so code that integrates with other WooCommerce extensions usually gets skipped or baselined. <strong>Ján Mikláš</strong> introduces <a href=\"https://developer.woocommerce.com/2026/11/03/php-stubs-for-extensions/\"><strong>automated PHP stubs for Woo extensions</strong></a>, a <code>woocommerce/php-stubs</code> package generated from extensions on the WooCommerce Marketplace and WordPress.org. The stubs are public on GitHub and Packagist. If your plugin or theme talks to other Woo extensions, you can check those calls without installing every extension in your analysis environment.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WooCommerce 11.3 fixes a confusing count for stores that sell bundles. When an extension adds a product to the cart as part of a bundle, the Product Button says &#8220;1 in cart&#8221; even though the shopper never added it on its own. <strong>Luis Herranz</strong> explains <a href=\"https://developer.woocommerce.com/2026/10/27/cart-item-children/\"><strong>declaring parent-child cart item relationships</strong></a> through a new <code>woocommerce_store_api_cart_item_parent_item_key</code> filter and a <code>parent_item_key</code> field in the Store API. WooCommerce never marks items as children by itself, so it&#8217;s up to your extension to declare them. Today only the button count uses it, but the Cart and Mini-Cart blocks could someday nest child items under their parents.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"what-s-new-in-playground\"><strong>What&#8217;s new in Playground?</strong></h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">You can now test changes on a copy of your real site without a local server. <strong>Fellyph Cintra</strong> details how to <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/playground/2026/11/04/clone-your-wordpress-site-in-playground-with-reprint/\"><strong>clone your WordPress site in Playground with Reprint</strong></a>. Install the Reprint plugin on your live site, authorize Playground with a migration key, and you&#8217;ll have an editable copy in your browser. Nothing you change there gets published to your live site. It&#8217;s handy for trying a design change, checking a plugin update, or tracking down a bug. Not ready to connect your own site? The post links to a public demo you can clone first.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Playground&#8217;s building blocks aren&#8217;t only for WordPress anymore. In <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/playground/2026/10/23/joomla-playground-shared-php-tools-beyond-wordpress/\"><strong>Joomla Playground: shared PHP tools beyond WordPress</strong></a>, <strong>Fellyph Cintra</strong> looks at Herman Peeren&#8217;s community project, which runs Joomla in a browser tab on the same <code>@php-wasm</code> packages and <code>mysql-on-sqlite</code> library. The interesting part is what comes back to WordPress. A different CMS sends different queries and workloads through those shared tools, surfacing compatibility and performance issues both projects can fix.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\" id=\"ai-in-wordpress\"><strong>AI in WordPress</strong></h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Imagine telling your AI agent, &#8220;Help me with my example.com site,&#8221; and it recognizes WordPress, asks you to authorize it, and gets to work. That&#8217;s the experience behind <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/ai/2026/10/28/wordpress-for-the-agentic-web/\"><strong>WordPress for the Agentic Web</strong></a>, the AI team&#8217;s plan from <strong>Jason Adams</strong>. It lays out four areas of work: a more reliable MCP Adapter that could move into Core, a baseline set of Abilities for common site tasks, OAuth so you don&#8217;t have to paste Application Passwords into an agent, and an end-to-end user experience tying them together. </p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The 7.2 cycle lays the foundation, and 7.3 is the target. Each area still needs a lead, so now&#8217;s the time to raise your hand. Not everyone in the comments is sold on MCP-first, though. <strong>Adrian Mörchen</strong> argued that most agents already work fine over the REST API and that filling its gaps should come first, and Jason replied that Abilities serve both routes. The <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/ai/2026/11/02/ai-contributor-weekly-summary-28-october-2026/\"><strong>October 28 contributor summary</strong></a> covers the first discussion, with <strong>Tammie Lister</strong> volunteering to lead UX, and The Repository has <a href=\"https://www.therepository.email/wordpress-ai-team-lays-out-ambitious-four-part-plan-to-make-wordpress-agent-ready-by-7-3\"><strong>its own take on the plan</strong></a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Six new experiments landed in <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/ai/2026/11/02/whats-new-in-ai-1-5-0/\"><strong>AI 1.5.0</strong></a>, the canonical AI plugin, and <strong>Jeffrey Paul</strong> breaks down each one. Text to Speech generates an audio version of a post, and Content Verification detects C2PA Content Credentials in uploaded images. MCP Access installs the MCP Adapter and adds a <strong>Tools → MCP Access</strong> screen where you decide which Abilities agents can see. The other three are WebMCP, Revision Change Summary, and Block Container Label Generation. If you&#8217;re following the Agentic Web work, MCP Access is the one to try first.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Through the end of November, free WordPress.com sites can <a href=\"https://wordpress.com/blog/2026/11/03/free-chatgpt-claude/\"><strong>connect to ChatGPT or Claude at no cost</strong></a>. Full access normally comes with a paid plan. <strong>Alexa Peduzzi</strong> notes that both connections run on MCP, so after a one-time setup you can ask your AI to save a draft, clear out comments, or suggest what to fix this week. The post includes ready-made prompts that open in either tool. <em>Not interested?</em> Connecting is entirely optional, and a WordPress.com reply in the comments points to the separate <strong>Prevent third-party sharing</strong> setting for anyone worried about AI training on their content.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">AI prototypes that ignore the design system keep landing on designers&#8217; desks. In <a href=\"https://automattic.design/2026/10/27/working-towards-higher-quality-agentic-experiences/\"><strong>working towards higher-quality agentic experiences</strong></a>, <strong>@leilabyron</strong> shares the 13 best practices Automattic Design wrote for agentic interfaces, grouped under capturing intent, showing the work, and building trust. The guidance doesn&#8217;t stop at a list: an agentic-design-brief skill picks the practices that matter for a feature, and an AGENTS.md file gives coding agents explicit rules. One rule worth stealing: ask when the request is genuinely ambiguous, and don&#8217;t ask when the answer is already clear.</p>\n\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">What happens when an AI feature gives a wrong answer or lets a prompt injection slip through? At WordPress Day for AI Faro 2026, <strong>Ivelina Dimova</strong> shared how to use <a href=\"https://wordpress.tv/2026/10/28/ai-with-boundaries-implementing-guardrails-and-evals-in-wordpress/\"><strong>guardrails and evals for AI features in WordPress</strong></a> to test quality before anything reaches production. Anyone adding AI features to client sites should pair it with the Automattic Design post above. The slides are linked from the video page.</p>\n\n\n<table cellpadding=\"0\" cellspacing=\"0\" class=\"ng-block-0f511dfd52e9714e wp-block-newsletterglue-container ng-block\" style=\"color: #666666;\" width=\"100%\">\n<tbody>\n<tr>\n<td class=\"ng-block-vs ng-block-vs-1\" colspan=\"3\" height=\"0\" style=\"height: 0px;\"></td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td class=\"ng-block-hs ng-block-hs-1\" height=\"0\" style=\"width: 0px;\"></td>\n<td align=\"none\" class=\"ng-block-td\" style=\"font-size: 16px; There is no need for a test site locally or on a server. Have you been using it? <a href=\"mailto:pauli@gutenbergtimes.com\">Email me </a>with your experience.</p>\n</td>\n</tr>\n</tbody>\n</table>\n</td>\n<td class=\"ng-block-hs ng-block-hs-2\" height=\"0\" style=\"width: 0px;\"></td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td class=\"ng-block-vs ng-block-vs-2\" colspan=\"3\" height=\"0\" style=\"height: 0px;\"></td>\n</tr>\n</tbody>\n</table>\n\n\n<hr class=\"wp-block-separator has-alpha-channel-opacity is-style-wide is-style-wide--1\" />\n\n\n\n<p class=\"has-text-align-center has-small-font-size wp-block-paragraph\"><em>Questions? Community\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:4:\"guid\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:40:\"https://central.wordcamp.org/?p=16691960\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:4:\"link\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:123:\"https://central.wordcamp.org/news/2026/11/a-shoutout-to-meahadi-hasan-from-the-classroom-to-the-global-wordpress-community/\";s:7:\"attribs\";a:0:{}s:8:\"xml_base\";s:0:\"\";s:17:\"xml_base_explicit\";b:0;s:8:\"xml_lang\";s:0:\"\";}}s:11:\"description\";a:1:{i:0;a:5:{s:4:\"data\";s:12476:\"<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Learning becomes more meaningful when students get the opportunity to put their knowledge into practice.</strong> For <a href=\"https://profiles.wordpress.org/meahadi/\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">Meahadi Hasan</a>, WordPress Credits opened the door to opportunities beyond website development. He explored photography contributions, participated in community initiatives like Campus Connect, and contributed to Bengali translations through the Polyglots and Training Teams, becoming an active part of the global WordPress community.</p>\n\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-columns is-layout-flex wp-container-core-columns-is-layout-8f761849 wp-block-columns-is-layout-flex\">\n<div class=\"wp-block-column is-vertically-aligned-center is-layout-flow wp-block-column-is-layout-flow\">\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Meahadi is a final-year Computer Science and Engineering student at <a href=\"https://pundrauniversity.ac.bd/\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">Pundra University of Science &amp; Technology</a>, Bangladesh. He joined the WordPress Credits Program in August 2026 as part of his university&#8217;s Industrial Training (Internship) course. On 2 November 2026, he successfully completed the program, but his journey was about much more than earning a certificate.<br /></p>\n</div>\n\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-column is-layout-flow wp-block-column-is-layout-flow\">\n<figure class=\"wp-block-image size-full\"><a href=\"https://central.wordcamp.org/files/2026/11/meahadi.png\"><img alt=\"\" class=\"wp-image-16691961\" height=\"224\" src=\"https://central.wordcamp.org/files/2026/11/meahadi.png\" width=\"224\" /></a></figure>\n</div>\n</div>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\"><strong>Rediscovering WordPress and Exploring New Opportunities</strong></h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Meahadi first encountered WordPress in 2020 through YouTube tutorials, where he learned to build a basic website. However, he didn&#8217;t continue working with it regularly.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Joining WordPress Credits gave him an opportunity to explore WordPress from a completely different perspective. He discovered that WordPress is not just a website-building platform; it is a global open-source community where people with different skills can contribute.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">He chose four contribution teams that matched his interests: Photos, Polyglots, Community and Training.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\"><strong>66 Photographs and Contributions Beyond Development</strong></h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">One of Meahadi&#8217;s most remarkable achievements was his contribution to the WordPress Photo Directory.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">He initially set a target of publishing 30 photographs. He went beyond that goal and successfully published <strong>66 original CC0-licensed photographs</strong>, featuring nature, plants, landscapes, and everyday scenes from Bangladesh.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>A Selection of Meahadi’s Photo Contributions</strong></p>\n\n\n\n<figure class=\"wp-block-gallery has-nested-images columns-default is-cropped wp-block-gallery-2 He also:</strong></p>\n\n\n\n<ul class=\"wp-block-list\">\n<li>Contributed Bengali translation suggestions through the Polyglots Team.</li>\n\n\n\n<li>Prepared a Bengali translation of the Learn WordPress lesson <em>The WordPress Ecosystem</em>, currently available for review.</li>\n\n\n\n<li>Built his personal website at <a href=\"https://meahadi.blog/\">meahadi.blog</a></li>\n\n\n\n<li>Participated in WordPress Slack, GitHub, and Make WordPress community discussions.</li>\n</ul>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">These experiences helped him understand how people with different skills collaborate to make WordPress better.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading has-text-align-center has-white-color has-text-color has-link-color wp-elements-16\"><strong>1</strong></h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Portfolio Created</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>WordPress.Com</strong></p>\n</div>\n</div>\n</div>\n\n\n\n<div class=\"wp-block-spacer\" style=\"height: 35px;\"></div>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\"><strong>Learning Through Mentorship and Community</strong></h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Throughout the program, Meahadi received guidance from his assigned mentor, <strong><a href=\"https://profiles.wordpress.org/wprashed/\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">Rashed Hossain</a></strong>, who helped him navigate the learning process and contribution workflows.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong><a href=\"https://profiles.wordpress.org/devmuhib/\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">Muhibul Haque</a></strong> also shared useful resources and advice, particularly around the Training Team.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Through practical assignments, documentation, community discussions, and real contributions, Meahadi developed confidence in WordPress, translation workflows, documentation, and communicating with contributors.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Looking back on his experience, he shared that he previously viewed WordPress mainly as a platform for building websites. Now, he understands the many opportunities available within its global community, even beyond writing code.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\"><strong>What&#8217;s Next?</strong></h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Completing WordPress Credits is not the end of Meahadi&#8217;s journey.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">He plans to continue contributing photographs, improve his Bengali translation skills, explore more Learn WordPress localization opportunities, and participate more actively in the WordPress community.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">His advice to future students is simple: start with a contribution team that matches your interests, read the handbooks, ask questions, and focus on small, consistent contributions.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">As he reminds us, the biggest value of WordPress Credits is not just completing a course; it&#8217;s becoming part of a community where your contributions can be genuinely useful.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\"><strong>Celebrating More Than Course Completion</strong></h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Meahadi&#8217;s journey reflects what we hope to achieve through WordPress Credits: helping students move beyond theoretical learning, develop practical skills, discover their potential, and become active members of the global WordPress community.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">His story shows that you don&#8217;t have to be a developer to make a meaningful contribution. Whether through photography, translation, community, or development, there is a place for everyone in WordPress.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Congratulations, Meahadi, on completing WordPress Credits and making your mark in the WordPress community.
