class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:00:19] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Welcome to the Jukebox podcast from WP Tavern. My name is Nathan Wrigley.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Jukebox is a podcast which is dedicated to all things WordPress. The people, the events, the plugins, the blocks, the themes, and in this case, building partnerships between WordPress product makers and hosting companies.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you&#8217;d like to subscribe to the podcast, you can do that by searching for WP Tavern in your podcast player of choice, or by going to wptavern.com/feed/podcast, and you can copy that URL into most podcast players.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you have a topic that you&#8217;d like us to feature on the podcast, I&#8217;m keen to hear from you and hopefully get you, or your idea, featured on the show. Head to wptavern.com/contact/jukebox, and use the form there.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So on the podcast today we have Martin Schmidt.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Martin has been deeply involved in the WordPress and hosting industries since 2005 when he created his first WordPress travel blog while living in New Zealand. Over the years, he&#8217;s developed websites for small businesses, spent 13 years with IONOS in various management and strategy roles, and built partnerships at Wix, and a German SaaS company.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Most recently, Martin has focused on connecting WordPress plugin companies with web hosts, helping both sides unlock growth through smarter partnerships.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Today, the conversation focused on the often overlooked world of partnerships between plugin developers and hosting companies. Martin shares insights from his two decades of experience on why hosting providers are the perfect allies for WordPress product makers looking to grow, given their established relationships and marketing channels to large customer bases.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">We talk about the curious fact that many plugin builders put enormous effort into their products, but stumble when it comes to sales, marketing, and building the business relationships necessary for broader success.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Martin discusses what hosting companies are actually looking for in a partnership, the technical and marketing preparations plugin makers should have ready, and why empathy and preparation make all the difference when trying to connect with busy product managers.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you&#8217;re a developer, freelancer, or agency owner interested in taking your WordPress product to the next level, or if you want a candid look at how partnerships really work in the open source ecosystem, this episode is for you.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you&#8217;re interested in finding out more, you can find all of the links in the show notes by heading to wptavern.com/podcast, where you&#8217;ll find all the other episodes as well.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And so without further delay, I bring you Martin Schmidt.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I am joined on the podcast by Martin Schmidt. Great to be here.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:03:16] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Yeah, thank you. I&#8217;m not entirely sure.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:03:23] <strong>Martin Schmidt:</strong> I was LinkedIn. Yeah. How to partner with various different entities, possibly hosting companies are going to come up and things like that, so.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Before we begin that conversation, Martin are you able to just give us your little bio? I&#8217;m from Germany. I lived down south in Karlsruhe. Originally from the Cologne area. Started working with WordPress actually in I think 2005, something like that. When I was moving to New Zealand and started studying in New Zealand. That&#8217;s when I spun up my first WordPress site as a travel blog. And this was my first contact with WordPress. So I think, yeah, one of the, not one of the first users, but one of the very early.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:04:20] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Fairly early, yeah, pretty early.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:04:22] <strong>Martin Schmidt:</strong> Very early. So that&#8217;s when you still had to like download WordPress and upload it via FTP, and there was no like auto update in the WordPress admin, and stuff like that. So one of the very early users and.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Yeah, around that time, that&#8217;s also how I got in touch with like the whole hosting industry. Another four days later an interview in Karlsruhe. And then I started, I think two weeks later. There was, during the COVID pandemic, I moved employers and started to work for Wix, and spent two years there building partnerships in, Europe.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Actually also put them in touch with a company called Sevdesk, which is a German accounting company, similar to Zero or QuickBooks Online. And couple of months later the CEO asked me if I was interested in joining their side, after they had raised some money and built partnerships from their side. I kept him waiting a little bit, but then after a while I decided, that&#8217;s interesting to join a German SaaS company. Something different than hosting, and online presence.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So yeah, I spent the next two years building the ecosystem around the SaaS software, like all the software that integrates with the accounting product basically.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Then end of 24, I got a call from my former boss from Ionos, who had just moved to hosting.com and became the CRO, and they also wanted to do partnerships. So, yeah, had a chat with him and with the CEO and started there in February 2025, and started building partnerships for hosting.com.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And, the main focus was really on building partnerships with WordPress companies. Just after Cloudfest, we launched the partnership with WP Rocket. Started to build quite a few more partnerships, or that were in the making. We want to work with hosting companies.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">We&#8217;re really glad to be working with you. We would also love to work with a lot of more hosting companies, but honestly we don&#8217;t really know where to start, and how to do this. And they really got me thinking. And then over New Years, and you know when you like, get some time to maybe reflect on the year and stuff that&#8217;s happening, this was like, it, really hit me. It&#8217;s like, well there&#8217;s so much interest of companies wanting to partner with web hosts.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And I&#8217;ve sat on the other side for some 20 years, like people pitching to me, like explaining me why their product is going to be the next big thing for me in the hosting company. And you start to see patterns of like things that people do good, and maybe things that people don&#8217;t do as well when like trying to pitch to you as a hosting company.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And I saw all this demand from these WordPress plugin companies and I was like, hey, maybe this is finally the idea of starting my own business. Like something I wanted to do for the last five years, but I never really had the idea of like what to do? Or not the idea that was convincing enough for myself to give up my corporate job.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And, once you got a house and family, I mean, it becomes a bit more difficult to just jump into the cold water, right? I guess a lot of people can relate to that. But this time it was different. I felt like this could really be something. So I started reaching out to more people in my network, and ask them, hey, I&#8217;m thinking about doing like this sort of consulting advisory thing. Do you think this is interesting? And I got a lot of positive feedback, way more than I had anticipated.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So I thought, okay, if I&#8217;m not doing this now, I will never do it. I will never try and do it. So yeah, I decided to go independent and officially started in May. And that&#8217;s what I&#8217;m doing now, like helping tech companies who want to partner with web hosts to find a way to do it.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And I always tell people that are interested in, you can do it by yourself, right? You can find it all out yourself. It&#8217;s probably going to take you like three to four years to figure out the right model, and make all the connections. Build the trust with the hosting companies. Find out the right business model. The right support model, like the co-marketing activities, all the things you need to do. Or if you don&#8217;t want to wait for four years, you can work with me and I help you take a shortcut.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And the goal is by month six that you have someone in your organisation who can take care of partnerships, and just run this by yourself. But you don&#8217;t make all of those mistakes, and all of those extra investments that don&#8217;t turn into anything.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">My pitch to my customers is like, I&#8217;m helping you cut down the time to market. I&#8217;m telling you what works and what doesn&#8217;t work, and what makes a good pitch.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:09:49] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Yeah, this is genuinely really interesting to me, and we&#8217;ll get into that a little bit later. But I have a different angle to you, but probably we&#8217;ll end up at exactly the same place.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I&#8217;ve been podcasting in the WordPress space for a decade. So 2016, something like that, I started. And, if you do podcasting often enough, your voice is heard, and people have this sort of expectation, if you send an email to me with a product that you&#8217;ve just launched, I will somehow have a silver bullet for making you successful.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And first thing to say is, I basically don&#8217;t. You know, I can put you on a podcast episode, but that really is the limits of what I can do.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">But also what&#8217;s quite fascinating, and I honestly genuinely, I probably get about 30 or 40 of these emails a week. And the bit that stands out for me is how unprepared, let&#8217;s go for that word, how poorly prepared the email that I receive is.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So in many cases over the last couple of years, it&#8217;s clearly been written by an AI. There hasn&#8217;t been a lot of due diligence about who I am, so it kind of feels like this really, really cold call. I somehow got on an email list somehow. And it comes, you know, with all of the usual caveats. The first paragraph is telling me what I do, and I already know what I do. So like there was an AI in there somewhere.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">But also it feels like they&#8217;ve got a product. Quite often I&#8217;ll go and look at the product and I think to myself, boy, that&#8217;s so good. Look at what they&#8217;ve done. Like they&#8217;ve built a plugin and it&#8217;s fulfilling a real need.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Unfortunately, I can&#8217;t really help them except put them on a podcast, but it occurs to me if only they could connect with all the right people, have the right language, know how to pitch themselves, know how their website should look so that it speaks to people that drop into it.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">What I&#8217;m basically trying to build up a picture is of is that there&#8217;s loads of people out there who are building things, but clearly the building of it is the part that they&#8217;ve been interested in. And as soon as it&#8217;s built, they then have this moment of, oh, now what do I do with it? You know? That&#8217;s honestly what a lot of companies think.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And that&#8217;s the difficulty that a lot of partner managers have, right? They&#8217;re being sent out to get those partners, and then they&#8217;re expected to bring in just all the revenue without any additional investment. And that&#8217;s just not happening. Honest truth, nobody is out there waiting for you and for your product.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:32:32] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Yeah. That is the brutal truth isn&#8217;t it? Do you subscribe to doing things in person? You know, so WordPress has got a real panoply of events, Meetups and WordCamps and there&#8217;s obviously other things that you mentioned, you know, Cloudfest and things like that. Do you find that tends to short circuit things?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">I guess there&#8217;s a bit of personality that comes into this as well. You know, some people are, seem to have all the characteristics for selling. They&#8217;re incredibly good at that kind of thing. Maybe they&#8217;re practised at it. Maybe they&#8217;re just, you know, born in that wonderful manner. Do you subscribe to that? Is that a part of this jigsaw puzzle as well? You know, show up, meet the people, shake the hands, go to the restaurants, do the schmoozing and all of that.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:33:12] <strong>Martin Schmidt:</strong> Absolutely. You could do all the Zoom calls that you want, but I&#8217;ve experienced it firsthand. Like during COVID, we were talking to people for six months via video conference. Like one in person meeting in Munich. It felt like this moved us further than just the six months of constant video calls.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">You can meet people, and you can relate to them, and there can be some sympathy and stuff like that. And you might not have to meet ever, but it usually helps a lot to build that trust. To just meet the person face to face, shake a hand, maybe have a beer and have a laugh and stuff like that just helps a great deal.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">And I&#8217;ve seen partnerships without any face-to-face meeting, but usually I would definitely advise to see people in person. And some people are just difficult to get hold of. And then catching them at the Colosseum Bar at Cloudfest 10 o&#8217;clock at night really helps to suddenly be in touch with that person.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:34:07] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> There is something ever so human about that isn&#8217;t there? And I think we all on some level enjoy that.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">So you found yourself in this product market space. Like I said, I know that this is a thing. So I would imagine that given a fair wind, you&#8217;ll be doing this for years to come.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Where do you find yourself online? Do you have a website where you&#8217;ve packaged up these products and services, you know, these partnership liaison services that you do? Where can people find you? Where do they go online to discover more about you?</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:34:37] <strong>Martin Schmidt:</strong> At the moment mostly active on LinkedIn. That&#8217;s where I meet a lot of the people. I also have a website, martinschmidt.co. And that&#8217;s where also people can reach out to me. The website has been a little bit neglected to be quite fair because I straight on, jumped into LinkedIn and started publishing on LinkedIn, because the audience is already there. And people are not searching for how do I build partnerships with web hosts? But they come to me via LinkedIn because they tend to read some of my posts, and that&#8217;s how I get to speak to people like yourself.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:35:07] <strong>Nathan Wrigley:</strong> Yeah. Well, that is exactly how it happened. It was LinkedIn and I think you&#8217;re right. I think having a website would be of great value, but at the same time, you know, given the proclivities of what you&#8217;re doing, LinkedIn seems to be very much the best place to do that.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">What I&#8217;ll do is I will put your bits and pieces into the show notes. So if dear listener, you head to wptavern.com you search for the episode with Martin Schmidt, that&#8217;s S-C-H-M-I-D-T, you&#8217;ll be able to find the links there. So hopefully you&#8217;ll be able to contact Martin via LinkedIn and what have you.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Really interesting topic and Martin, thank you for chatting to me about it today.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">[00:35:42] <strong>Martin Schmidt:</strong> Yes. Thanks for having me, Nathan. It was fantastic.</p>\n</div></details>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">On the podcast today we have <a href=\"https://www.martinschmidt.co\">Martin Schmidt</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Martin has been deeply involved in the WordPress and hosting industries since 2005, when he created his first WordPress travel blog while living in New Zealand. Over the years, he’s developed websites for small businesses, spent 13 years with IONOS in various management and strategy roles, and built partnerships at Wix and a German SaaS company. Most recently, Martin has focused on connecting WordPress plugin companies with web hosts, helping both sides unlock growth through smarter partnerships.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Today the conversation focused on the often-overlooked world of partnerships between plugin developers and hosting companies. Martin shares insights from his two decades of experience on why hosting providers are the perfect allies for WordPress product makers looking to grow, given their established relationships and marketing channels to large customer bases.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">We talk about the curious fact that many plugin builders put enormous effort into their products, but stumble when it comes to sales, marketing, and building the business relationships necessary for broader success.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Martin discusses what hosting companies are actually looking for in a partnership, the technical and marketing preparations plugin makers should have ready, and why empathy and preparation make all the difference when trying to connect with busy product managers.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you’re a developer, freelancer, or agency owner interested in taking your WordPress product to the next level, or if you want a candid look at how partnerships really work in the open source ecosystem, this episode is for you.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Useful links</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><a Please do not install, run, or test this version of WordPress on production or mission-critical websites. Instead, use a test environment or local site to explore the new features.</p>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Wait, what happened to 7.2 Beta 3???</h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><a href=\"https://wordpress.org/news/2026/10/wordpress-7-1-4/\">WordPress 7.1.4 was released on October 28</a> which included WordPress 7.2 Beta 3.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">How to Test WordPress 7.2 Beta 4</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">You can test WordPress 7.2 Beta 4 in any of the following ways:</p>\n\n\n\n<figure class=\"wp-block-table\"><table class=\"has-fixed-layout\"><tbody><tr><td><strong>WordPress Beta Tester Plugin</strong></td><td>Install and activate the <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/plugins/wordpress-beta-tester/\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\" target=\"_blank\">WordPress Beta Tester</a> plugin on a WordPress install. Select the “Bleeding edge” channel and “Beta/RC Only” stream.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Direct Download</strong></td><td>Download the <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/wordpress-7.2-beta4.zip\">Beta 4 version (zip)</a> and install it on a WordPress website.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Command Line (WP-CLI)</strong></td><td>Use this <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/cli/\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\" target=\"_blank\">WP-CLI</a> command: <br /><code>wp core update --version=7.2-beta4</code></td></tr><tr><td><strong>WordPress Playground</strong></td><td>Use a <a href=\"https://playground.wordpress.net/release\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">7.2 Beta 4 WordPress Playground instance</a> to test the software directly in your browser. No setup required-just click and go!</td></tr></tbody></table></figure>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The scheduled final release date for WordPress 7.2 is <strong><strong>December 8, 2026</strong></strong>. The full <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/core/2026/10/06/wordpress-7-2-release-party-schedule/\">release schedule can be found here</a>. Your help testing Beta and RC versions is vital to making this release as stable and powerful as possible. Thank you to everyone who contributes by testing!</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">What&#8217;s in WordPress 7.2 Beta 4?</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">For technical details on the issues addressed since Beta 2, see the following links:</p>\n\n\n\n<ul class=\"wp-block-list\">\n<li><a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/query?status=closed&amp;changetime=10%2F27%2F2026..11%2F03%2F2026&amp;milestone=7.2&amp;group=component&amp;col=id&amp;col=milestone&amp;col=owner&amp;col=type&amp;col=priority&amp;order=id\">Closed 7.2 WordPress Core Trac tickets</a> since October 27, 2026</li>\n\n\n\n<li><a href=\"https://github.com/WordPress/gutenberg/commits/wp/7.2?since=2026-10-27&amp;until=2026-11-03\">7.2 Gutenberg commits</a> since October 27, 2026</li>\n</ul>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Continued Improvements to WordPress 7.2</h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress 7.2 Beta 4 continues refining the upcoming release with improvements to accessibility, editor reliability, and developer APIs, alongside additional bug fixes and compatibility updates.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Recent work includes improved keyboard focus visibility in Windows High Contrast mode, refinements to administration interfaces, and fixes for right-to-left block alignment and custom block spacing.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Developers will also benefit from continued improvements to the Abilities API, DataViews extensibility, and REST API behavior. Additional fixes strengthen revision handling, input validation, and compatibility across supported environments.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Learn more:</strong> <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/45910\">Trac #45910</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66382\">Trac #66382</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66427\">Trac #66427</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66383\">Trac #66383</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66343\">Trac #66343</a>, <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66329\">Trac #66329</a></p>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Continued Improvements to Ipsum</h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">The new default theme, Ipsum, continues to receive refinements ahead of WordPress 7.2&#8217;s final release. Recent updates improve accessibility with clearer keyboard focus indicators, more descriptive screen reader links, and better support for right-to-left languages. Additional improvements refine block patterns, touch target spacing, and color palette compatibility with other default themes.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Learn more:</strong> <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/66352\">Trac #66352</a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/WordPress/wordpress-develop/pull/15113\">GitHub #15113</a></p>\n\n\n\n<h3 class=\"wp-block-heading\">Improved Translation Update Management</h3>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">WordPress 7.2 makes managing translation updates easier by displaying individual available updates on the Updates screen. Administrators can now review and selectively install translation updates, bringing language management in line with the familiar plugin and theme update experience.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><strong>Learn more:</strong> <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/ticket/42281\">Trac #42281</a>, <a href=\"https://github.com/WordPress/wordpress-develop/pull/11610\">GitHub #11610</a></p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">How important is your testing?</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Testing for issues is a critical part of developing any software, and it’s a meaningful way for anyone to contribute – whether or not you have experience. <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/test/2026/10/20/help-test-wordpress-7-2/\">Details on what to test in WordPress 7.2 are available here</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">If you encounter an issue, please share it in the <a href=\"https://wordpress.org/support/forum/alphabeta/\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">Alpha/Beta area</a> of the support forums. If you are comfortable submitting a reproducible bug report, you can do so <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/newticket\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">via WordPress Trac</a>. You can also check your issue against this list of <a href=\"https://core.trac.wordpress.org/tickets/major\" rel=\"noopener\" target=\"_blank\">known bugs</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\">Curious about testing releases in general and how to get started? Follow along with the <a href=\"https://make.wordpress.org/test/\">testing initiatives in Make Core</a> and join the <a href=\"https://wordpress.slack.com/messages/core-test/\">#core-test channel</a> on <a href=\"https://wordpress.slack.com/\">Making WordPress Slack</a>.</p>\n\n\n\n<h2 class=\"wp-block-heading\">A Beta 4 haiku</h2>\n\n\n\n<p class=\"wp-block-paragraph\"><em>Where did Beta Three ) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

--
-- Table structure for table `wp_posts`
--

CREATE TABLE `wp_posts` (
  `ID` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
  `post_author` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `post_date` datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_date_gmt` datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_content` longtext NOT NULL,
  `post_title` text NOT NULL,
  `post_excerpt` text NOT NULL,
  `post_status` varchar(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'publish',
  `comment_status` varchar(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
  `ping_status` varchar(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
  `post_password` varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `post_name` varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `to_ping` text NOT NULL,
  `pinged` text NOT NULL,
  `post_modified` datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_modified_gmt` datetime NOT NULL DEFAULT '0000-00-00 00:00:00',
  `post_content_filtered` longtext NOT NULL,
  `post_parent` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `guid` varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `menu_order` int(11) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `post_type` varchar(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'post',
  `post_mime_type` varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `comment_count` bigint(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

--
-- Table structure for table `wp_termmeta`
--

CREATE TABLE `wp_termmeta` (
  `meta_id` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
  `term_id` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `meta_key` varchar(255) DEFAULT NULL,
  `meta_value` longtext DEFAULT NULL
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

-- --------------------------------------------------------

--
-- Table structure for table `wp_terms`
--

CREATE TABLE `wp_terms` (
  `term_id` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
  `name` varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `slug` varchar(200) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `term_group` bigint(10) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

-- Dumping data for table `wp_terms`

) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

-- Table structure for table `wp_term_taxonomy`

  `taxonomy` varchar(32) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `description` longtext NOT NULL,
  `parent` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `count` bigint(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;

CREATE TABLE `wp_users` (
  `ID` bigint(20) UNSIGNED NOT NULL,
  `user_login` varchar(60) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_pass` varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_nicename` varchar(50) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_email` varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_url` varchar(100) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',

  `user_activation_key` varchar(255) NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
  `user_status` int(11) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  `display_name` varchar(250) NOT NULL DEFAULT ''
) ENGINE=InnoDB DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_unicode_520_ci;
